// src/services/rotate-aws-secrets.test.ts

import { describe, it, expect, beforeEach, afterAll } from "vitest";

import { mockClient } from "aws-sdk-client-mock";

import {

SecretsManagerClient,

PutSecretValueCommand,

GetSecretValueCommand,

} from "@aws-sdk/client-secrets-manager";

import { handler } from "./rotate-aws-secrets";

const secretsManagerMock = mockClient(SecretsManagerClient);

// Helper to create legacy secret mock

const mockLegacySecret = {

ENCRYPTION_KEY: "old-enc-key-base64",

HMAC_KEY: "old-hmac-key-base64",

};

// Helper to create versioned secret mock

const mockVersionedSecret = {

version: 2,

current: {

    ENCRYPTION_KEY: "current-enc-key-base64",

    HMAC_KEY: "current-hmac-key-base64",

},

previous: {

    ENCRYPTION_KEY: "previous-enc-key-base64",

    HMAC_KEY: "previous-hmac-key-base64",

},

};

describe("rotate-aws-secrets handler", () => {

const originalEnv = process.env;

const validSecretArn =

    "arn:aws:secretsmanager:us-east-1:123456789:secret:argus-keys";

beforeEach(() => {

    secretsManagerMock.reset();

    process.env = { ...originalEnv };

    process.env.SECRET_ARN = validSecretArn;

});

afterAll(() => {

    process.env = originalEnv;

    secretsManagerMock.restore();

});

describe("successful rotation", () => {

    it("should rotate secrets successfully", async () => {

      secretsManagerMock.on(GetSecretValueCommand).resolves({

        SecretString: JSON.stringify(mockLegacySecret),

      });

      secretsManagerMock.on(PutSecretValueCommand).resolves({});


      await expect(handler()).resolves.not.toThrow();

    });


    it("should call PutSecretValueCommand with correct SecretId", async () => {

      secretsManagerMock.on(GetSecretValueCommand).resolves({

        SecretString: JSON.stringify(mockLegacySecret),

      });

      secretsManagerMock.on(PutSecretValueCommand).resolves({});


      await handler();


      const calls = secretsManagerMock.commandCalls(PutSecretValueCommand);

      expect(calls).toHaveLength(1);

      expect(calls[0].args[0].input.SecretId).toBe(validSecretArn);

    });


    it("should generate new versioned secret structure (AR-28)", async () => {

      secretsManagerMock.on(GetSecretValueCommand).resolves({

        SecretString: JSON.stringify(mockLegacySecret),

      });

      secretsManagerMock.on(PutSecretValueCommand).resolves({});


      await handler();


      const calls = secretsManagerMock.commandCalls(PutSecretValueCommand);

      const secretString = calls[0].args[0].input.SecretString;

      const secrets = JSON.parse(secretString!);


      // New versioned structure

      expect(secrets).toHaveProperty("version", 2); // 1 (legacy) + 1

      expect(secrets).toHaveProperty("current");

      expect(secrets.current).toHaveProperty("ENCRYPTION_KEY");

      expect(secrets.current).toHaveProperty("HMAC_KEY");

      expect(secrets).toHaveProperty("previous");

      expect(secrets).toHaveProperty("rotatedAt");

    });


    it("should preserve previous keys during rotation (AR-28)", async () => {

      secretsManagerMock.on(GetSecretValueCommand).resolves({

        SecretString: JSON.stringify(mockLegacySecret),

      });

      secretsManagerMock.on(PutSecretValueCommand).resolves({});


      await handler();


      const calls = secretsManagerMock.commandCalls(PutSecretValueCommand);

      const secrets = JSON.parse(calls[0].args[0].input.SecretString!);


      // Previous should contain the old keys

      expect(secrets.previous.ENCRYPTION_KEY).toBe(

        mockLegacySecret.ENCRYPTION_KEY,

      );

      expect(secrets.previous.HMAC_KEY).toBe(mockLegacySecret.HMAC_KEY);

    });


    it("should increment version when rotating versioned secret", async () => {

      secretsManagerMock.on(GetSecretValueCommand).resolves({

        SecretString: JSON.stringify(mockVersionedSecret),

      });

      secretsManagerMock.on(PutSecretValueCommand).resolves({});


      await handler();


      const calls = secretsManagerMock.commandCalls(PutSecretValueCommand);

      const secrets = JSON.parse(calls[0].args[0].input.SecretString!);


      expect(secrets.version).toBe(3); // 2 + 1

      expect(secrets.previous.ENCRYPTION_KEY).toBe(

        mockVersionedSecret.current.ENCRYPTION_KEY,

      );

    });


    it("should generate base64 encoded 256-bit keys", async () => {

      secretsManagerMock.on(GetSecretValueCommand).resolves({

        SecretString: JSON.stringify(mockLegacySecret),

      });

      secretsManagerMock.on(PutSecretValueCommand).resolves({});


      await handler();


      const calls = secretsManagerMock.commandCalls(PutSecretValueCommand);

      const secretString = calls[0].args[0].input.SecretString;

      const secrets = JSON.parse(secretString!);


      // Base64 encoded 32 bytes = 44 characters (with padding)

      expect(secrets.current.ENCRYPTION_KEY).toMatch(/^[A-Za-z0-9+/]+=*$/);

      expect(secrets.current.HMAC_KEY).toMatch(/^[A-Za-z0-9+/]+=*$/);


      // Verify they decode to 32 bytes

      const encKeyBuffer = Buffer.from(

        secrets.current.ENCRYPTION_KEY,

        "base64",

      );

      const hmacKeyBuffer = Buffer.from(secrets.current.HMAC_KEY, "base64");

      expect(encKeyBuffer.length).toBe(32);

      expect(hmacKeyBuffer.length).toBe(32);

    });


    it("should include ISO timestamp in rotatedAt", async () => {

      secretsManagerMock.on(GetSecretValueCommand).resolves({

        SecretString: JSON.stringify(mockLegacySecret),

      });

      secretsManagerMock.on(PutSecretValueCommand).resolves({});


      await handler();


      const calls = secretsManagerMock.commandCalls(PutSecretValueCommand);

      const secretString = calls[0].args[0].input.SecretString;

      const secrets = JSON.parse(secretString!);


      // Verify rotatedAt is a valid ISO date string

      const rotatedAt = new Date(secrets.rotatedAt);

      expect(rotatedAt.toISOString()).toBe(secrets.rotatedAt);

    });


    it("should generate unique keys on each rotation", async () => {

      secretsManagerMock.on(GetSecretValueCommand).resolves({

        SecretString: JSON.stringify(mockLegacySecret),

      });

      secretsManagerMock.on(PutSecretValueCommand).resolves({});


      // First rotation

      await handler();

      const calls1 = secretsManagerMock.commandCalls(PutSecretValueCommand);

      const secrets1 = JSON.parse(calls1[0].args[0].input.SecretString!);


      // Reset mock for second rotation (return the result of first rotation)

      secretsManagerMock.reset();

      secretsManagerMock.on(GetSecretValueCommand).resolves({

        SecretString: JSON.stringify(secrets1),

      });

      secretsManagerMock.on(PutSecretValueCommand).resolves({});


      // Second rotation

      await handler();

      const calls2 = secretsManagerMock.commandCalls(PutSecretValueCommand);

      const secrets2 = JSON.parse(calls2[0].args[0].input.SecretString!);


      // Keys should be different

      expect(secrets1.current.ENCRYPTION_KEY).not.toBe(

        secrets2.current.ENCRYPTION_KEY,

      );

      expect(secrets1.current.HMAC_KEY).not.toBe(secrets2.current.HMAC_KEY);

    });

});

describe("error handling", () => {

    it("should throw error when SECRET_ARN is not set", async () => {

      delete process.env.SECRET_ARN;


      await expect(handler()).rejects.toThrow(

        "SECRET_ARN environment variable is not set",

      );

    });


    it("should throw error when SECRET_ARN is empty string", async () => {

      process.env.SECRET_ARN = "";


      await expect(handler()).rejects.toThrow(

        "SECRET_ARN environment variable is not set",

      );

    });


    it("should throw error when GetSecretValueCommand fails", async () => {

      const awsError = new Error("Access denied");

      secretsManagerMock.on(GetSecretValueCommand).rejects(awsError);


      await expect(handler()).rejects.toThrow("Access denied");

    });


    it("should throw error when PutSecretValueCommand fails", async () => {

      secretsManagerMock.on(GetSecretValueCommand).resolves({

        SecretString: JSON.stringify(mockLegacySecret),

      });

      const awsError = new Error("Access denied");

      secretsManagerMock.on(PutSecretValueCommand).rejects(awsError);


      await expect(handler()).rejects.toThrow("Access denied");

    });

});

});

// src/services/rotate-aws-secrets.ts

import {

SecretsManagerClient,

PutSecretValueCommand,

GetSecretValueCommand,

} from "@aws-sdk/client-secrets-manager";

import { Logger } from "@aws-lambda-powertools/logger";

import { randomBytes } from "crypto";

import type { VersionedSecrets } from "./get-aws-secrets";

const logger = new Logger({ serviceName: "argus-secrets-rotator" });

const client = new SecretsManagerClient({});

/\*\*

- Legacy flat secrets structure

\*/

interface LegacySecrets {

ENCRYPTION_KEY: string;

HMAC_KEY: string;

version?: string;

}

/\*\*

- Manual secret rotation utility with key versioning (AR-28).

-

- IMPORTANT: This preserves the previous key during rotation to prevent

- split-brain issues where some Lambda instances have cached old keys.

- Data encrypted with the previous key remains readable for one full

- cache duration (15 minutes) after rotation.

-

- The rotation process:

- 1.  Fetch current secret

- 2.  Move current keys to "previous"

- 3.  Generate new keys as "current"

- 4.  Increment version number

- 5.  Update secret with both keys

\*/

export const handler = async (): Promise<void> => {

const secretArn = process.env.SECRET_ARN;

if (!secretArn) {

    throw new Error("SECRET_ARN environment variable is not set");

}

try {

    // Fetch existing secret to preserve current keys as previous

    const getCommand = new GetSecretValueCommand({ SecretId: secretArn });

    const existingData = await client.send(getCommand);

    const existingSecret = JSON.parse(existingData.SecretString!) as

      | VersionedSecrets

      | LegacySecrets;


    // Extract current keys from either versioned or legacy format

    let currentKeys: { ENCRYPTION_KEY: string; HMAC_KEY: string };

    let currentVersion: number;


    if ("current" in existingSecret && existingSecret.current) {

      // Versioned format

      currentKeys = existingSecret.current;

      currentVersion =

        typeof existingSecret.version === "number" ? existingSecret.version : 1;

    } else {

      // Legacy flat format

      const legacy = existingSecret as LegacySecrets;

      currentKeys = {

        ENCRYPTION_KEY: legacy.ENCRYPTION_KEY,

        HMAC_KEY: legacy.HMAC_KEY,

      };

      currentVersion = 1;

    }


    // Generate new versioned secret structure

    const newSecrets: VersionedSecrets & { rotatedAt: string } = {

      version: currentVersion + 1,

      current: {

        ENCRYPTION_KEY: randomBytes(32).toString("base64"), // 256-bit AES key

        HMAC_KEY: randomBytes(32).toString("base64"), // 256-bit HMAC key

      },

      previous: currentKeys, // Preserve current keys as previous

      rotatedAt: new Date().toISOString(),

    };


    // Update the secret

    await client.send(

      new PutSecretValueCommand({

        SecretId: secretArn,

        SecretString: JSON.stringify(newSecrets),

      }),

    );


    logger.info("Secret rotated successfully", {

      secretArn,

      rotatedAt: newSecrets.rotatedAt,

      version: newSecrets.version,

    });

} catch (error) {

    logger.error("Failed to rotate secret", { error });

    throw error;

}

};

Please critique.

Don't be a sycophant or pharisee - look for hidden footguns, or things that _will_ blow up in my face at scale.

Ultra Think.

This is a sophisticated, well-structured codebase. You have clearly read the AWS whitepapers and implemented the "Right Way" patterns (CDK, Powertools, Dependency Injection).

However, at 30 Billion requests/year, "clean code" doesn't save you from physics or AWS limits.

Here are the hidden footguns and architectural flaws that will explode when you hit production load.

1. The Tier 2 "Hot Partition" Suicide Pact (P0)

You are using DynamoDB for Tier 2 matching (Fuzzy/Compound matching) using an Adjacency List pattern where PK = bucket_key.

The Footgun: DynamoDB partitions data by PK. A single partition can handle ~1,000 WCUs and ~3,000 RCUs per second. Your buckets are defined as: tenant#gpu_screen_tz#.... Consider the bucket: tenant1#gpu_screen_tz#iPhone14_Renderer#390x844#America/New_York.

At 30B requests/year, you have ~1,000 requests/second average. If a popular device configuration (iPhone in NYC) represents even 5% of your traffic:

    That single partition receives 50 writes/second (Fine).

    BUT, for matching, you Query that partition. If you are doing 50 reads/second on that partition, and each Query scans 1,000 items (your TIER2_BUCKET_LIMIT), you are consuming 50,000 RCUs/second on a single partition key.

The Explosion: AWS will throttle that partition immediately. Your Tier 2 queries will time out (TIER2_TIMEOUT_MS = 100). You will fail open to "New Device". You will generate millions of duplicate Device IDs for your most common users.

Fix: Do not use DynamoDB for high-cardinality bucketing queries. This specific pattern requires a search engine (OpenSearch) or a sharded Redis set. DynamoDB cannot handle the Read Amplification of querying a popular bucket at this scale. 2. The Key Rotation "Split-Brain" Data Loss (P1)

You implemented versioned secrets in src/config/env.ts and src/services/get-aws-secrets.ts, but you missed the critical fallback logic in the consumption layer.

The Footgun:

    Rotator Lambda runs. Secret moves from v1 to v2.

    Ingestion Service (Go) polls secrets every X minutes. It picks up v2. It starts encrypting TCP/TLS blobs with v2.

    Matching Worker (Node) caches secrets for KEY_CACHE_DURATION (15 minutes). It still holds v1.

    The Gap: For up to 15 minutes, your Workers cannot decrypt payloads sent by Ingestion. They will throw errors.

    The Blast Radius: SQS retries will kick in. If the queue backlog is < 15 minutes, the messages will fail retry counts and DLQ. You lose data.

Fix: Your decryption logic must attempt to decrypt with current key, catch the error, and try previous key. The code provided in src/handlers/matching-worker.ts does not show this fallback logic inside the fingerprint payload processing. 3. The "Cost of Cleanliness" (Financial Footgun)

You are running a Go Service on Fargate behind an ALB for ingestion.

The Math:

    30B requests/year.

    ALB LCU charges are complex, but generally expensive for high connection counts (websockets/fingerprinting).

    Fargate tasks cost money per vCPU/GB.

    NAT Gateway: Your Fargate tasks and Lambdas are in Private Subnets. They send 30B requests to SQS/DynamoDB. Even with VPC Endpoints, you pay for data processing.

The Comparison: An API Gateway HTTP API (not REST) costs $1.00/million requests.

    30,000 million * $1.00 = $30,000/year.

    Your current Fargate + ALB + NAT setup at 30B scale will likely exceed this when you factor in auto-scaling over-provisioning and ALB LCU costs for SSL termination.

Critique: You built a custom Go ingestion layer to do validation that API Gateway VTL or Lambda could do. It adds a massive operational burden (patching, scaling policies) for marginal gain. 4. SQS "ReportBatchItemFailures" Trap

In src/handlers/matching-worker.ts:
TypeScript

batchItemFailures.push({ itemIdentifier: record.messageId });

The Footgun: When you return a partial batch failure to SQS, the failed messages are kept in the queue. They become visible again after VisibilityTimeout. If your Redis cluster blips or DynamoDB throttles (see point #1), every item in the batch fails. SQS puts them back. The Lambda scales up to process the backlog. The underlying resource is still stressed. The retries compound the pressure.

The Explosion: You have created a retry storm. Unlike a DLQ which removes bad data, ReportBatchItemFailures keeps "poison pills" (data that causes timeouts) in the hot path until maxReceiveCount is hit.

Fix: Implement a "Backoff" inside the Lambda or use a lower maxReceiveCount on the main queue to move failing items to DLQ faster during outages. 5. Redis "Volatile-LRU" is dangerous for Session Caching

In lib/constructs/redis.ts, you set:
TypeScript

"maxmemory-policy": "volatile-lru",

The Footgun: You use Redis for Tier 0 (Session Cache) and Mutation Gates. If Redis fills up (likely, given 30B req/year implies massive concurrency), it starts evicting keys with TTLs.

    Session Cache Eviction: User refreshes page -> Cache Miss -> Full re-processing -> Higher DB load. (Acceptable).

    Mutation Gate Eviction: Gate key evicted -> Request proceeds -> Write Amplification.

If you are under a DDoS or heavy load, your Redis fills up. LRU kicks in. It deletes Mutation Gates. Now every request hits DynamoDB to update the profile. You just amplified the attack on your own database.

Fix: Use separate Redis instances (or logical databases) for "Cache" (disposable) and "Gates/Locks" (critical for DB protection). 6. Minor Nits & Code Smells

    fs in Lambda? In ingestion-service.ts, you use path.join. Ensure you aren't trying to read local files in the Lambda environment that aren't bundled. (Looks okay here as it's CDK time, but watch out).

    Testing Mocks vs Reality: Your tests use ioredis-mock. It behaves perfectly. Real Redis has connection timeouts, packet loss, and ECONNRESET. Your getRedis function has retry logic, but ensure maxRetriesPerRequest isn't so high that a Lambda times out waiting for Redis, causing an SQS retry.

Summary Verdict

    Architecture: B+ (Good separation, but Tier 2 DB design is fatal).

    Code Quality: A (Very clean, strict types).

    Scalability: C (DynamoDB hot partitions and Redis eviction strategy will fail at 30B scale).

Immediate Actions:

    Kill Tier 2 Query: Switch Tier 2 matching to a search-optimized store or remove it. It cannot survive on DynamoDB.

    Verify Decryption Fallback: Ensure MatchingWorker can decrypt with previous key.

    Split Redis: Prioritize locking keys over cache keys.

So what are the waterflow steps?

1. see if cookie exists in hot redis cache

2. See if rare hash combos exist, then pick the one closes to the target

3. vector db?

Please explain how this thing flows

This is how the waterfall matching logic flows in your codebase (src/services/matching/matching-service.ts).

It is designed as a sieve: it tries the fastest, most precise methods first (Tier 0), and falls back to slower, fuzzier methods (Tier 2/3) only if necessary.
The Waterfall Flow

1. Tier 0: The "Hot" Cache (Redis)

Goal: Instant return for active users.

    Input: session_id from the request.

    Action: Checks Redis key session:{session_id}.

    Correction to your premise: It does not check if a "cookie" exists in Redis. It checks if this specific browsing session has already been matched.

    Result: If found, return device_id immediately (Latency: ~2ms).

2. Tier 0.5: The "Immutable" Anchor (DynamoDB)

Goal: Catch users who cleared cache but not LocalStorage/Evercookies.

    Input: fingerprint.evercookie_id (if present).

    Action: Checks Tier1Index table for PK: evercookie#{id}.

    Result: If found, return match with 99% confidence.

3. Tier 1: The "Rare Hash" Exact Match (DynamoDB)

Goal: Match devices with identical hardware/browser signatures.

    Input: stable_hash (a hash of strict signals like Canvas + Audio + Hardware).

    Action: Checks Tier1Index table for PK: stable#{hash}.

    Result: If found, return match with 95% confidence.

    Fallback: If stable_hash fails, it tries fuzzy_hash (a hash of looser signals). If found, return match with 85% confidence.

4. Tier 2: The "Compound Bucket" Search (DynamoDB)

Goal: Catch devices that drifted slightly (e.g., browser update changed the Canvas hash).

    Input: Combinations of weak signals (e.g., IP + JA4, or GPU + Screen + Timezone).

    Action:

        Generates 3 "Bucket Keys" (e.g., tenant#ip_ja4#1.2.3.4#hash).

        Queries the Tier2Buckets table for all 3 keys in parallel.

        Scoring: It looks for a device_id that appears in multiple buckets. (e.g., "This device matched the IP bucket AND the GPU bucket").

    Result: Returns the device with the highest overlap score. Confidence varies (60-85%).

    Note: This step has a hard 100ms timeout. If DynamoDB is slow, it aborts and "fails open."

5. Tier 3: Vector Similarity (Future / TODO)

   Goal: Semantic matching using embeddings.

   Status: Currently a comment in your code (// TODO: implement when Qdrant is deployed).

   Action: Would query a Vector DB (Qdrant) for the nearest neighbor to the fingerprint vector.

6. Tier 4: New Device Generation

Goal: The catch-all.

    Action: If all above steps fail (or Tier 2 times out), generate a new UUID (dev_...).

    Result: Return match with 100% confidence (it is definitely a new device ID, because we just made it up).

Visual Summary

B -- Yes --> C[Return Cached DeviceID]
B -- No --> D{DynamoDB: Evercookie exists?}
D -- Yes --> E[Return Match 99% Conf]
D -- No --> F{DynamoDB: Stable Hash exists?}

F -- No --> H{DynamoDB: Fuzzy Hash exists?}
H -- Yes --> I[Return Match 85% Conf]
H -- No --> J{DynamoDB: Compound Buckets?}
J -- Match Found --> K[Return Best Candidate 60-85%]
J -- No Match / Timeout --> L[Vector DB Search]
L -.-> M[TODO: Not Implemented]
L --> N[Generate NEW DeviceID]

When this thing is bootstrapping, won't everyone hit the vectordb?

Yes, you are absolutely correct. You just identified the single biggest "success disaster" in this architecture.

If you deploy this with the Vector DB enabled in the synchronous path (Tier 3), and then turn on traffic (bootstrapping), 100% of your users will be "misses" in Tiers 0, 1, and 2.

This means 100% of your traffic will hammer the Vector DB simultaneously looking for a nearest neighbor that doesn't exist yet.
The "Cold Start" Waterfall of Death

When the system is empty, the logic flows like water down a drain—straight to the bottom.

    Tier 0 (Redis): Empty. MISS.

    Tier 1 (DynamoDB Hashes): Empty. MISS.

    Tier 2 (DynamoDB Buckets): Empty. Query returns 0 items immediately. MISS.

    Tier 3 (Vector DB): HIT. (Every single request executes a vector search).

Vector searches are CPU-intensive (calculating cosine similarity across high-dimensional space). If you throw 1,000 requests/second at a cold Qdrant/Pinecone/OpenSearch cluster, it will fall over instantly. Latency will spike to seconds, timing out your API.

Why You Are Safe (For Now)

Looking at src/services/matching/matching-service.ts:
TypeScript

// Tier 3: Vector similarity (TODO: implement when Qdrant is deployed)

// New device - no match found
return {
result: this.createNewDevice(),
tier2TimedOut: timedOut,
};

Currently, Tier 3 is commented out. Your code currently falls through Tier 2 and immediately hits createNewDevice(), which is just a CPU UUID generation (fast).
The Fix: Don't Block on Vectors

When you do implement the Vector DB, do not put it in the synchronous matching path (MatchingWorker).

The Async Pattern:

    Matching Worker (Sync): Tries Tier 0 → Tier 1 → Tier 2. If no match, return a New Device ID immediately to the client.

    Profile Worker (Async): In the background (via SQS), take that "New" ID and fingerprint vector. Query the Vector DB.

        If Match Found: "Merge" the profiles. Mark the "New" ID as an alias of the "Found" ID in your database.

        If No Match: Write the new vector to the DB.

Why this works:

    The user gets a 204 response instantly (20ms).

    The expensive Vector Search happens in the background.

    If the Vector DB is overwhelmed, the SQS queue just backs up; the user experience is unaffected.

Rule of Thumb: Never put a probabilistic, heavy search (like Vector or fuzzy regex) in the critical path of a high-throughput API unless you have massive over-provisioning.

THe handler just throws data into SQS. The client never waits.

So how do I handle bootstrapping or a ton of first time visitors that fall through level 1,2,3; and hit the expensive 4 and 5 - and miss; then need to get ETL'd into the DB

You have hit on the exact reason why Async (SQS) architectures are dangerous if not tuned correctly. You are correct: the client is gone (204 response). They are happy.

But now you have 10 million messages in your SQS queue, and your Lambda workers are waking up to process them.

If 100% of those messages fall through to the expensive tiers (Vector/Tier 2) because the DB is empty, you will create a "Brownout" where your backend infrastructure melts down trying to process the backlog.

Here is exactly how you handle the "Cold Start / Bootstrapping" phase without bankrupting yourself or destroying your database.

1. The Safety Valve: Lambda Concurrency Limits

This is your primary defense. You must set reservedConcurrentExecutions on your MatchingWorker Lambda.

    Scenario: 100,000 users hit your site in 1 minute.

    Without Limit: AWS spins up 1,000+ Lambda instances. They all hit your Vector DB/DynamoDB simultaneously. Result: Database crashes/throttles.

    With Limit (e.g., 50): AWS spins up exactly 50 Lambdas. They process 50 messages at a time. The other 99,950 messages sit safely in the SQS queue.

    Outcome: Your database only ever sees 50 concurrent connections. It stays healthy. The queue drains steadily over time.

2. The "Bootstrap Mode" Strategy (Feature Flags)

When you first launch (or turn on Vector DB), do not turn on the Reads.

Use environment variables to disable the expensive "Read" paths while keeping the "Write" paths active.

Phase 1: Hydration (Weeks 1-4)

    Matching Logic:

        Tier 1 (Hash): ON (Fast, efficient).

        Tier 2 (Compound): OFF (Skip the expensive query).

        Tier 3 (Vector): OFF (Skip the expensive query).

        Result: If Tier 1 misses, immediately return "New Device".

    Profile Logic (Writes):

        ON: Write the new device to DynamoDB Tier 2 and Vector DB.

    Effect: You are filling your databases with data (Hydrating) without paying the penalty of querying empty buckets.

Phase 2: Activation (Week 5+)

    Once you have populated 10M+ devices, flip the env vars to ON.

    Now, when a user comes in, there is actually data to find, making the expensive queries worth it.

3. The "Fail Fast" Circuit Breaker

If you must run with expensive tiers enabled from Day 1, you need a circuit breaker.

In src/services/matching/matching-service.ts:
TypeScript

// Pseudo-code for Circuit Breaker
async tier2CompoundMatch(...) {
// 1. Check if Tier 2 is "open" (healthy)
if (this.circuitBreaker.isOpen("tier2")) {
return null; // Skip immediately, fallback to New Device
}

try {
// 2. Do the expensive query
const result = await this.queryDynamoTier2(...);
this.circuitBreaker.recordSuccess();
return result;
} catch (error) {
// 3. If timeout/error, record failure
this.circuitBreaker.recordFailure();
return null;
}
}

If DynamoDB/Vector DB starts timing out (because of the thundering herd), the circuit breaker "opens" and skips that tier for the next 30 seconds. Traffic flows straight to "New Device" (Tier 4), which is just CPU generation (fast). 4. "New Device" Creation is Cheap

You mentioned "miss; then need to get ETL'd into the DB".

Actually, Creating a New Device is the cheapest path.

    Matching Worker: Generates UUID. Writes to Redis. (2ms). Done.

    Profile Worker (Async): Takes the new ID. Writes to DynamoDB.

        This happens after the match is returned.

        If your SQS profile_queue backs up with 10M "New Device" writes, who cares?

        The user is already browsing. The fraud check (Match) is finished. The "ETL" (Write) can take 2 hours to catch up, and nobody will notice.

Summary: The Bootstrapping Game Plan

    Set Concurrency: Limit MatchingWorker to 50 to protect your DBs. Let the SQS queue absorb the spike.

    Disable Expensive Reads: Set ENABLE_TIER2_MATCHING=false and ENABLE_VECTOR_MATCHING=false for the first month.

    Let Writes Happen: Allow the Profile Worker to populate your indexes in the background.

    Accept Duplicates: During this phase, you will create some duplicate IDs for the same user (because Tier 2 is off). This is acceptable cost for system stability during launch.

So there's a hydration phase where it sounds like you could _literally_ use an ETL approach - with feature flags, just let all the data fall through to an S3 bucket and ETL to build the vector-db and any dynamo data necessary. After that, turn on a couple of the L1/2/3 filters, letting whatever falls through become subject to the next round of ETL, which updates the dynamos and vector-db's. After maybe a month, you can let it go WOT and you _should_ be ok?

Yes, exactly. You have described a "Dark Launch" or "Warm-Up Strategy."

This is the standard way to launch search, recommendation, or fraud engines without setting money on fire. You decouple the Data Collection (Writes) from the Data Usage (Reads).

Here is the refined battle plan for your hydration phase using the S3 ETL approach you suggested.
Phase 1: The "Data Vacuum" (Weeks 1-2)

Goal: Collect data cheaply. Don't look anything up.

    Traffic: 100% of live traffic.

    Matching Worker:

        Tier 1-3 Reads: OFF (Feature Flag).

        Action: Immediately returns New Device ID. (Fastest possible path).

    Profile Worker:

        DynamoDB Writes: ON. (DynamoDB handles simple PutItem writes easily at scale. Let it fill up Tier1Index and Tier2Buckets live. It handles the write load fine).

        Firehose: ON. Dump every fingerprint to S3 (Parquet/JSON).

    Vector DB: OFF (Don't even spin up the cluster).

Result: Your users get 20ms response times. Your DynamoDB fills with millions of "New" devices. S3 builds a massive training dataset.
Phase 2: The "Offline Build" (Week 3)

Goal: Build the expensive indexes without affecting live traffic.

    Action: Spin up a temporary batch job (AWS Glue or a beefy EC2).

    Source: The S3 bucket from Phase 1.

    Task:

        Read all fingerprints.

        Generate Embeddings.

        Bulk Load them into your Vector DB (Qdrant/Pinecone).

    Why this wins: Bulk loading a Vector DB is vastly more efficient (and produces better HNSW graphs) than incremental random writes.

Phase 3: The "Tiered Rollout" (Week 4)

Goal: Gradually enable reads to verify Match Rates.

    Step A (Tier 1): Enable Tier 1 (Hash) Reads.

        Expectation: You should immediately see 30-40% "Cache Hits" for returning users.

        Cost: Low (O(1) lookups).

    Step B (Tier 2): Enable Tier 2 (Compound) Reads.

        Expectation: Catch the "drifters." Match rate goes up.

        Risk: Monitor DynamoDB read throttling. This is your canary.

    Step C (Vector): Enable Vector Reads (only for those who fail Tier 1 & 2).

        Traffic: By now, only ~20% of traffic should fall through to this expensive layer.

Phase 4: WOT (Wide Open Throttle)

Goal: Business as usual.

    You are now "Ok" because:

        Cache Warm: Redis and DynamoDB are populated. Most users hit a "cheap" match early in the waterfall.

        Backpressure Tested: You've seen the write load on DynamoDB during Phase 1.

        Vector Safety: You aren't hammering the Vector DB with 100% of traffic, only the subset that genuinely needs deep inspection.

Summary of the "ETL Approach" Benefits
Feature "Naive" Launch "ETL / Dark" Launch
Vector DB Load 100% of requests (Death) ~20% of requests (Manageable)
Index Quality Fragmented (Random writes) Optimized (Bulk built)
User Latency High/Timeouts Constant Low (20ms)
Cost High (Paying for misses) Low (Only paying for hits)

One correction to your plan: Don't use ETL for DynamoDB data. Let the Profile Worker write to DynamoDB live during Phase 1. DynamoDB is designed for high-concurrency writes. It saves you the complexity of writing a "DynamoDB Backfill" script later. Use the S3 ETL purely for the Vector DB / Analytics side.

So to re-iterate, the flow goes like this:

1. Session-Id (redis)

2. Cookie-Id (dynamo)

3. Hash-Combos (dynamo)

4. Vector DB (qdrant|other-thing)

This is correct. You have the exact hierarchy of "fastest/cheapest" to "slowest/smartest."

Here is the flow confirmed:

    Session-Id (Redis)

        Speed: ~1-2ms

        Logic: "I have seen this specific session in the last 15 minutes."

        Action: Returns cached device_id instantly.

    Cookie-Id (DynamoDB - Tier 0.5)

        Speed: ~5-10ms

        Logic: "I recognize this Evercookie ID from a previous visit."

        Action: Returns device_id with 99% confidence.

    Hash-Combos (DynamoDB - Tier 1 & 2)

        Speed: ~10-20ms (Tier 1) / ~30-50ms (Tier 2)

        Logic:

            Tier 1: "This exact Stable Hash or Fuzzy Hash exists." (High confidence).

            Tier 2: "This IP+JA4 and GPU+Screen combination exists." (Medium confidence).

        Action: Returns device_id if a match is found.

    Vector DB (Qdrant - Tier 3)

        Speed: ~50-100ms+

        Logic: "This fingerprint 'looks like' one I've seen before, even though hashes don't match." (Semantic similarity).

        Action: Returns nearest neighbor device_id if similarity > threshold.

If all 4 fail: You generate a New Device ID.
Summary of the "Hydration/Dark Launch" Strategy

    Phase 1 (Hydration): Turn OFF Steps 2, 3, and 4 for Reads.

        Let everyone fall through to "New Device ID".

        Write the data to DynamoDB and S3 (for Vector ETL) in the background.

        Result: Fast performance, data accumulates.

    Phase 2 (Activation): Turn ON Step 2 & 3.

        Now you get cache hits on Cookies and Hashes.

        Result: Match rates improve, cost stays low.

    Phase 3 (Vector): Bulk-load the Vector DB from S3 data. Turn ON Step 4.

        Only the hardest cases reach this step.

        Result: Maximum fraud detection capability, protected from "cold start" scaling death.

This plan gives you a safe path to 30B requests without melting your infrastructure on Day 1.

Worth setting up a bloom-filter(s) around 2 or 3? It'll tell us if they exist or not, saving us from hitting 2 Dynamo queries - if that makes a difference

It depends heavily on which Tier you are protecting.

The short answer:

    Tier 3 (Vector DB): No. It is mathematically impossible.

    Tier 2 (DynamoDB Buckets): Maybe, but the operational pain usually outweighs the cost savings.

    Tier 1 (Exact Hash): Yes, this is actually the best place for it.

Here is the breakdown of why.

1. Tier 3 (Vector DB): The "Similarity" Trap

Verdict: Don't do it.

A Bloom filter answers one question: "Have I seen this exact string before?" A Vector DB answers a different question: "Do I have anything similar to this?"

    The Logic Gap: If a user reaches Tier 3, they have already failed Tier 1.

        Tier 1 failure means: "We have never seen this exact fingerprint hash before."

        Therefore, a Bloom filter on the exact fingerprint would always return "No."

        But you still need to query the Vector DB to see if a "neighbor" exists.

    Conclusion: A Bloom filter cannot "cache" similarity searches. You cannot skip the Vector DB lookup unless you've seen the exact fingerprint, which Tier 1 already handles.

2. Tier 2 (Dynamo Buckets): The "Delete" Problem

Verdict: High Complexity / Low Reward.

Tier 2 checks compound keys like tenant#ip_ja4#1.2.3.4#hash.

    The Benefit: If the Bloom Filter says "No" (definitely not in set), you save 3 DynamoDB Reads. At 30B requests, if 50% are new/unique, that saves billions of reads (~$10k-$15k/year).

    The Footgun: Bloom Filters generally do not support deletes.

        Your device profiles change. Buckets expire (TTL).

        Your Bloom Filter will just keep growing forever.

        As it fills up, the "False Positive" rate increases. Eventually, it says "Yes/Maybe" to everything, and you are back to hitting DynamoDB every time.

    The Fix (Over-Engineering): You would need Cuckoo Filters or Rotational Bloom Filters (e.g., a fresh filter for every day). This requires maintaining complex state in Redis.

    Recommendation: Not worth the engineering hours vs. the DynamoDB bill.

3. The "Sweet Spot": Tier 1 (Stable Hash)

Verdict: Worth it for "Miss" Heavy Traffic.

If your traffic has a lot of "New Users" or "Bots" (random fingerprints that never return), a Bloom filter in front of Tier 1 is highly effective.

    Scenario: A botnet hits you with 10M random fingerprints.

    Without Bloom: 10M reads to DynamoDB (GetItem). Cost: ~$2.50. Latency: 5-10ms each.

    With Bloom:

        Check Redis Bloom BF.EXISTS stable_hash.

        Result: "No".

        Action: Skip DynamoDB entirely. Return "New Device".

        Cost: Redis RAM. Latency: 1ms.

    Why this works: Tier 1 is your "Front Door." Blocking misses here saves the waterfall from ever starting.

Summary Recommendation

    Skip Bloom Filters for now.

        The "Hydration / Dark Launch" strategy solves your bootstrapping cost problem better than Bloom filters.

    Monitor "Miss Rate" in CloudWatch.

        If you see that 80%+ of your Tier 1 lookups are "Misses" (New Devices), then add a Redis Bloom Filter in front of Tier 1.

    Forget Tier 3 optimization.

        Vector search is expensive by nature; you can't shortcut the math.
